The Legacy and Evolution of Rockstar Games: How One Studio Redefined Open-World Gaming

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Usama Mehmood is a Senior Writer at DualShockers with more than five years of experience in the video game industry. He has been writing professionally since 2021 and covering games since 2022, with work spanning guides, lists, reviews, and features across action-adventure games, JRPGs, open-world titles, racing games,

I grew up with an unhealthy obsession with the Grand Theft Auto series. The game wasn’t obviously meant for an 8-year-old, but it showed me the greatest gift: my parents wanting to give me happiness and comfort throughout my upbringing, even if it meant unknowingly handing me a game that was clearly meant for older audiences.

But hey, it didn’t meddle with my behavior, alright? Playing San Andreas was one of my most endearing experiences, with countless playthroughs/save files. And I mean, some of my best video game memories even involved my dad helping me clear a couple of tough missions in both it and Vice City. So from those 3D-era entries to see how far Rockstar has come to be this industry powerhouse with their sandbox video game series is genuinely astounding to see.

Aaron Garbut, co-studio head of Rockstar North, explained that no single person has the entire game mapped out in their head.

Rockstar Games has spent decades building some of the most recognisable worlds in video game history. Whether it's the streets of Los Santos, the mob-boss-riddled underworld of Liberty City, or a quiet ride through the unforgiving American frontier with an on-the-run gang of outlaws, there's something remarkably special about the worlds this studio creates.

With Grand Theft Auto VI approaching in just over two weeks, it's worth looking back at how that journey unfolded. From the studio's transformation and the franchises that shaped its identity to the enormous expectations around its next release, Rockstar's legacy is about much more than the games it has sold. It's about how one developer helped redefine what players expect from open-world experiences.

We’re taking this back to the humble beginnings of Rockstar Games. See, before it became one of the most popular names in the gaming landscape, its story actually began with a much smaller development team in Dundee, Scotland. Founded in 1988 by David Jones and a group of fellow developers, DMA Design started as a relatively small studio with ambitions to make its mark in an industry still finding its footing.

Its early releases, including Menace and Blood Money, showed its interest in arcade-inspired action games, but the arrival of a quirky little game called Lemmings in 1991 truly put the developer on the map. This relatively simple puzzle game (unexpectedly) became an enormous success, selling an estimated 20 million copies across its various platforms.

However, a very different kind of project would ultimately change the studio's future, since in 1997, DMA Design would then release the original Grand Theft Auto, trading its familiar arcade experiences for a top-down crime sandbox that allowed you to steal vehicles, complete odd-job missions, evade the police, and wreak havoc across fictional cities.

As the OG Grand Theft Auto continued to gain momentum despite being an experimental first entry, DMA Design underwent a series of ownership changes before becoming part of Take-Two Interactive and the wider Rockstar Games family around the turn of the millennium.

Eventually, the studio relocated from Dundee to Edinburgh, and in 2002, it officially became Rockstar North. This transformation, or turnaround, whichever one you prefer, would soon carry forward the creative foundations established years earlier. And little did you or I know just how far that journey would take them; this small Scottish team was now at the heart of a studio that would go on to produce some of the most influential open-world experiences in gaming history.

Former Rockstar technical director Obbe Vermeij recalls how GTA 3’s small team made communication easier and gave developers room to experiment.

Funnily enough, I had the chance to revisit GTA 3 this past month as part of my replay of all the mainline entries before VI’s arrival. Forgive me if it was the Definitive Edition; it was dirt-cheap on sale, and I’d already played the OG version on my PS2 as a kid, so I thought, why not try things differently, especially after this much time has passed since this trilogy’s controversial release?

Now, contrary to what the header suggests, although open-world games existed long before Grand Theft Auto III arrived, few developers had managed to bring together the same level of freedom, exploration, and interactive storytelling that Rockstar would eventually become known for. In fact, this is also why I’d like to give a shout-out to my co-worker and friend, Maddie, for discussing how 1999's Urban Chaos, developed by Mucky Foot Productions, was already experimenting with a 3D urban environment, free-roaming exploration, and mission-based gameplay two years before GTA 3 hit the PS2.

While it wasn't the expansive sandbox Rockstar would then create in later titles, it showed the potential of this simple yet experimental idea: letting players explore and interact with a full 3D city. But because it went beyond the formulaic approach of the prior two GTAs, you could absolutely make a case that this game was a generational release for its time.

Instead of simply following a predetermined series of missions, players could steal vehicles, explore different neighborhoods, interact with pedestrians, engage in shootouts, and stumble upon all sorts of unexpected chaos along the way. Driving, shooting, and exploring were no longer separate gameplay modes, but seamlessly interconnected parts of a much larger experience.

Oh, and it didn’t stop there, obviously. The game's success would go on to influence countless developers, establishing a formula that would shape much of the open-world genre for years to come, since Rockstar wasn't simply offering players a large map to explore; it was creating an environment filled with activities, distractions, and opportunities for experimentation.

Playing San Andreas was one of my most endearing experiences, with countless playthroughs/save files.

The studio continued refining that approach with Grand Theft Auto: Vice City in 2002 and then Grand Theft Auto: San Andreas in 2004. The former introduced a distinctive 1980s atmosphere reminiscent of Miami Vice’s stylized tone and an unforgettable soundtrack, while the latter expanded the formula considerably with multiple cities, character customization, and RPG-inspired mechanics.

So by the time San Andreas arrived, Rockstar had shown how much freedom and variety could fit into a single open-world experience. It is genuinely insane that these guys released three back-to-back bangers; it's an honest travesty that they outsourced the modern re-release of them to an external developer under their umbrella.

GTA 3 often ran at just 7 or 8 FPS during development, according to former Rockstar technical director Obbe Vermeij.

While Grand Theft Auto remained Rockstar's biggest commercial success, the company was never particularly interested in limiting itself to a single franchise; these guys were seriously trailblazing their way to the top. That’s because throughout the 2000s, its growing network of development studios experimented with different genres, storytelling approaches, and settings, resulting in some of its most memorable cult favorites.

Bully took the familiar open-world formula and turned it into a crude yet charming coming-of-age adventure. Meanwhile, Manhunt explored a much darker side of the company's creativity through its disturbing atmosphere (enough to give me the frights as a kid) and brutal stealth-based gameplay, and then Midnight Club brought open-city exploration into the street racing scene of the 2000s that other series like NFS also capitalized on with Fast and Furious’ popularity.

And don’t worry, like most of you, possibly longtime fans reading this, I still hold an undying grudge against Rockstar for never revisiting these games again, especially the high possibilities for a sequel to Bully, which I’m never burying the hatchet over because the Scholarship Edition is one of my most played and adored games on the Xbox 360. The bottom line is that these projects showed Rockstar's ambitions extended well beyond crime-driven satire sandboxes, allowing its various studios to establish their own identities across genres.

Rockstar Games has spent decades building some of the most recognisable worlds in video game history.

Of course, Grand Theft Auto wasn't going anywhere. And how ironic that I rolled credits on this just a week ago. With GTA IV in 2008, Rockstar took a noticeably different approach to its established formula for a grittier, more grounded portrayal of Liberty City akin to New York City.

It introduced a string of themes like revenge, organized crime overlords butting heads for power, and the often uncomfortable realities of chasing the American Dream. Plus, things like the ragdoll physics and weight-based driving model were the icing on the cake for an otherwise brilliant crime drama, one that I personally think is the best in the series in terms of writing. However, as much as I hate to admit it, Rockstar wasn't finished pushing those boundaries.

An interview with Rockstar reveals details about GTA 6's morality system.

Grand Theft Auto V arrived in 2013, taking you back to Los Santos and introducing three playable protagonists: Michael, Franklin, and Trevor. Its intertwining storytelling, enormous variety of activities, and remarkable sense of freedom made it a defining open-world release of its generation. Today, it is insane to think about how many records this game has broken: it's one of the best-selling video games of all time, second only to Minecraft, and has generated billions of dollars since its original launch.

Beyond the story mode, GTA Online (unfortunately) gradually grew into a massive multiplayer platform of its own, keeping players invested through years of updates, new missions, vehicles, heists, and activities. And I won’t lie: as someone who spent an unhealthy number of hours on it, this game was partly why I found two of my bestest friends. Also, this mode's longevity and the revenue it generated through microtransactions were low-key part of why GTA V became a cultural phenomenon that kept thriving across multiple console generations.

The wizards at Rockstar didn’t stop there, because the leap that came forward after would soon set the benchmark for people to compare most open-world releases to, even if most of those comparisons became insufferable to come across in social media posts and/or videos.

And that considerable leap came when Rockstar dropped the technical bombshell that was Red Dead Redemption 2 in 2018. You see, I could’ve put the original entry in the section above with the other projects these guys did, but it only felt right to include it here, since both these two Wild West open-world titles became games that almost felt like companion pieces to the GTA franchise. That was evident not only in the mission design, but also in newer additions like the Honor system, a fully realized wildlife ecosystem, and other gameplay elements that made it feel like a sister game to GTA.

Following the success of the 2010 entry, the sequel offered a story centered around Arthur Morgan and the gradual decline of the Van der Linde gang, one that slowly connected to the events of RDR1. Its greatest achievement, however, was arguably the extraordinary level of detail packed into its bygone world. From real wildlife behavior and daily NPC routines to dynamic world events that shifted depending on the protagonist or time passed, almost every aspect of its world contributed to an incredible sense of immersion.

Although years later, you’ll now find some recent verbal engagements about people arguing about its flawed mission design, and as much as I think it's a bit overrated (please put the pitchforks down), I will wholeheartedly agree on the fact that this was THE game that made Rockstar the rightful heir to the throne for creating one of the most immersive and compelling open-world games of all time. Most importantly, it also rewarded those willing to slow down and simply enjoy their surroundings, and I think that’s the best compliment to give RDR2 given the current attention span of most mainstream audiences.

After decades of refining its approach to open-world design, Rockstar Games now stands on the doorstep of what could be its most ambitious project yet, as GTA VI represents more than just another long-awaited sequel, especially considering how much the studio has accomplished since we first stepped foot into Los Santos by repo’ing cars with Franklin and Lamar or just cruising around Ocean Beach as Tommy.

I won’t bore your ear with a bunch of what-if bumbo jumbo, but all I want to sign off with is that, given everything Rockstar has showcased so far, there's plenty of reason to be excited about the possibilities they could improve upon here, particularly when considering the technical artistry demonstrated in RDR2.

We’ve already seen an extent of the full gameplay in action with the Extended Look, as well as tons of deeper details shared online across media outlets that make this entry a proper sim-like experience, which I honestly don’t mind since I genuinely wanted Rockstar to bring back the RPG stuff they introduced back in San Andreas. Heck, even if it’s not confirmed, throw in vehicle schools again to give Lucia and Jason even more stats to manage.

For me, one of the biggest challenges GTA VI faces is delivering a story that can stand alongside GTA IV because Niko Bellic's journey for me remains one of the franchise's most memorable narratives precisely because it went beyond the usual criminal escapade trope. Most of all, it made its protagonist feel like more than another criminal navigating a fictional city, and I'd love to see Jason and Lucia achieve that in their own way.

Their relationship already gives Rockstar an interesting foundation to explore, but what matters most is whether the studio can make players genuinely care about the people behind all the chaos. Give me another round of NPC social links calling either protagonist just for a round of bowling or, y’know, give me reasons to interact with them again via their special abilities/benefits.

Now, with Leonida and the newly imagined Vice City waiting just around the corner, it feels like we're about to experience the next chapter in a legacy that has shaped how an entire generation experiences video games.

While I generally have mixed feelings about the overly eccentric fanbase, I’ll put that aside for now since I’m also proudly blasting Flower in Hurricane and Inner Light on repeat these days to itch my hype fever for the game’s release on November 19th. Whether Rockstar can deliver another landmark open-world experience remains to be seen, but hey, to anyone who made it here to see it happen, I hope we eat good with whatever they’ve cooked up over the past 13 years.

Rockstar says GTA 6 pre-loading begins November 10 and could require up to 212 GB, though some official pages still show the old date.

'Trials and Tribble-ations' at 30: The classic 'Star Trek: Deep Space Nine' episode that brought Captains Kirk and Sisko face-to-face

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

How "Forrest Gump", pizza, and a massive supporting cast of furballs came together to make one of "Star Trek"'s finest hours.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

When "Star Trek" celebrated its 60th anniversary in September, it was a strangely muted affair. Sure, there were fan events, screenings, and even an attempt at a world record for the biggest simultaneous Vulcan salute in history, but the lack of a "Doctor Who"-style birthday episode felt like a glaring omission. It was like everyone had shown up for the party, but the organizers had forgotten to book the headline entertainment.

Luckily, there were rather more fireworks for the show's 30th birthday, when both "Voyager" and "Deep Space Nine" marked the occasion in style. "Voyager" episode "Flashback" took Tuvok (Tim Russ) back to his days on the USS Excelsior bridge alongside Original Series vets Hikaru Sulu (George Takei) and Janice Rand (Grace Lee Whitney).

But it was "DS9"'s "Trials and Tribble-ations" that turned out to be the more memorable voyage to "Trek" past, as Captain Benjamin Sisko (Avery Brooks) — with the help of some then-state-of-the-art digital trickery — came face-to-face with a young Captain James T. Kirk (William Shatner).

While "Flashback" aired within days of "Trek"'s September 8 birthday, fans had to wait a little longer for "DS9"'s contribution to the celebrations. Showrunner Ira Steven Behr was keen to avoid disrupting the space station-set drama's story arc for a comedic diversion, meaning that "Trials and Tribble-ations" didn't debut until November 4, 1996.

In the wake of "Flashback" — not to mention Spock and Scotty's comebacks in "The Next Generation" stories "Unification" and "Relics" — the "DS9" writers' room had already decided they needed to take a very different approach.

Early on, "Battlestar Galactica" creator Ronald D. Moore pitched a follow-up to Original Series episode "A Piece of the Action" (aka the one where the residents of Iotia brought 1920s gangsters to life). In an extremely meta concept that predated "Galaxy Quest" by three years, the story would have taken the Enterprise crew to a planet of Kirk and Spock impersonators.

"For the thirtieth anniversary, you go back, and you're basically going back to the planet of 'Star Trek' fans," Moore recalled in "The Fifty-Year Mission" by Mark A. Altman and Edward Gross. "They have conventions; they know all the adventures. It's a phenomenon, and you could comment on 'Star Trek' as a whole. I thought it would be interesting. But [fellow writer] René Echevarria thought it was a dangerous show to do, that we would be talking down to the fans on some level, and perhaps it wasn't the best way to go."

Instead, Echevarria suggested telling a story in the vein of Woody Allen's "Zelig" or "Forrest Gump" (then a zeitgeisty, multi-Oscar-winning box-office smash) and integrating the "Deep Space Nine" crew with the events of an Original Series episode.

"I thought, 'What bigger treat could there be than to go back into the past and board the original Enterprise and have a mission there?'" Echevarria said in a 1996 making-of documentary. "And Ron immediately said, 'It's gotta be "The Trouble with Tribbles!"'"

"The Trouble with Tribbles", a 1967 episode from "Star Trek"'s second season, is one of the most famous in the franchise's history. As well as featuring some minor scuffles with Klingons, it sees the Enterprise and Deep Space Station K-7 infested with the eponymous tribbles — cute, cuddly furballs of calm that reproduce at a dangerously exponential rate.

In "Trials and Tribble-ations", a timey-wimey incident involving the Bajoran Orb of Time transports the USS Defiant (one of the best "Star Trek" spaceships) back to the era of Kirk and Spock's famous five-year mission.

As Sisko recounts the adventure to a pair of agents from Starfleet's Department of Temporal Investigations (named Dulmur and Lucsly in homage to "The X-Files"), we learn how he, Dax (Terry Farrell), O'Brien (Colm Meaney), and Bashir (Alexander Siddig) spent a day playing Original Series cosplay on Kirk's Enterprise. It's "Star Trek"'s answer to "Back to the Future: Part II", as the crew interacts with established events while taking pains not to mess with history.

Although most of the guest stars from "The Trouble with Tribbles" had passed away over the intervening decades, one actor who'd played a pivotal role was still around. By the time he turned up in "Star Trek", 20-something stand-up comedian Charlie Brill had already brushed shoulders with pop-culture legend, having shared the bill with the Beatles when they made their famous appearance on "The Ed Sullivan Show" in February 1964.

Three years later he'd swapped Lennon and McCartney for Kirk and Spock, playing a Klingon spy masquerading as human Arne Darvin, whose cover is ultimately blown by the famously Klingon-phobic tribbles. In the newer episode, the older Darvin travels back in time to assassinate Kirk, plotting his revenge with a bomb implanted in an unfortunate tribble. Thwarting the evil scheme — and preserving the timeline — forms the basis of Sisko's mission.

Brill's return was seemingly written in the stars. When planning the episode, Behr and the other writers met for lunch at Mulberry Street Pizza in Beverly Hills and noted that the actor was alive and still working in the business. Then, just as they were talking about him, they happened to spot Brill across the very same restaurant.

"What were the chances that we would be talking about this old TV episode from 30 years ago, and the guy who we were talking about to bring back on the show would be standing there ordering a slice of pizza?" Behr recalled at the Trek Talks telethon (via TrekMovie).

But however strong its story, "Trials and Tribble-ations" was always going to live and die by its recreation of ' 60s-era "Trek". The production team subsequently pulled out all the stops, with Behr suggesting at the time that it may have been the most expensive hour of TV ever made.

The actors were kitted out in era-appropriate primary-colored outfits and given '60s hairdos. The make-up team also recreated the appearance of the Original Series' Klingons, whose very different look — "We do not discuss it with outsiders," says Worf — would later be explained away in a "Star Trek: Enterprise" two-parter.

The late Walter Koenig (who'd featured as Chekov in "The Trouble with Tribbles") showed the younger officers how to flip open a communicator, and the stunt team choreographed fights to look like the cheesy, Western-inspired brawls of a bygone era.

Behind the scenes, production designer Michael Okuda and his team used a mixture of original plans and scans of the episodes to painstakingly recreate the sets, while the visual effects team constructed an exact half-scale replica of the original 11-foot Enterprise shooting model housed in the Smithsonian.

The biggest magic trick, however, was dependent on CG technology — then very much in its infancy — making everyone believe that Sisko and co were walking the same corridors as Kirk. VFX supervisor Gary Hutzel had provided proof-of-concept early in the process:

"He showed us a clip from the old show," said Behr. "We looked at it and said, 'Okay, that looks good, but what does that have to do with us?' And he said, 'Well, you see that guy coming in through the door? That's not from the 1967 episode. That's one of our people!'"

Shooting in front of blue- and green-screens, the actors' performances were meticulously matched to the Original Series episode. Early on, Moore had identified two sequences from "The Trouble with Tribbles" that would be perfect for selling the concept: Kirk dressing down a line-up of officers after a bar room brawl and Tribbles raining down on Kirk from a storage container.

O'Brien and Bashir were digitally pasted over the original actors in the former, while the latter was explained away by Sisko and Dax's madcap dash to find the elusive explosive tribble. In most other shots, the DS9ers became background observers in existing scenes.

But there were still debates to be had over whether the two captains should meet. "It led to one of the most vociferous arguments we've had," said Behr, a passionate advocate for the "yes" camp. "We were so deadlocked, and there was so much emotion in the air, we actually turned to the writer intern and said, ‘What do you think?’ End of stalemate. Of course, being an intern and being a smart guy, he sided with his executive producer. And life went on." (The scene in which Sisko hands Kirk a duty roster for approval was actually lifted from another Original Series episode, "Mirror, Mirror", with Brooks replacing Barbara Luna (Lt. Marlena Moreau) on the bridge.)

It was all worth the effort. "Trials and Tribble-ations" was the most-watched episode of "DS9"'s fifth season, bagged nominations for three Emmys and a Hugo, and is now widely regarded as one of the best "Trek" episodes of all time.

David Gerrold, who wrote "The Trouble with Tribbles" and appeared as an extra in "Trials and Tribble-ations", described it as "possibly the best episode of 'Star Trek' after the Original Series," and various members of the writers' room have described it as a highlight of their time on the final frontier.

"From the writers to the director to the lighting, costumes, the set, sound effects, visual effects — everybody really put in so much time on that episode to really recreate the original Enterprise in any way they possibly could," said Moore. "Everybody was staring at film clips, doing research into [prop/costume] materials. It was a mania, and everybody just threw themselves into it. They wanted to make it great. And it shows."

'The Trouble with Tribbles' and 'Trials and Tribble-ations' are available to stream on Paramount+ in both the US and UK.

Richard's love affair with outer space started when he saw the original "Star Wars" on TV aged four, and he spent much of the ’90s watching "Star Trek”, "Babylon 5” and “The X-Files" with his mum. After studying physics at university, he became a journalist, swapped science fact for science fiction, and hit the jackpot when he joined the team at SFX, the UK's biggest sci-fi and fantasy magazine. He liked it so much he stayed there for 12 years, four of them as editor.

He's since gone freelance and passes his time writing about "Star Wars", "Star Trek" and superheroes for the likes of SFX, Total Film, TechRadar and GamesRadar+. He has met five Doctors, two Starfleet captains and one Luke Skywalker, and once sat in the cockpit of "Red Dwarf"'s Starbug.

Huddersfield Town's draw with Luton: Two points dropped or another point in the right direction

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

The Terriers' intense head coach Martin Drury believes his players look on him at times as "a bit of an idiot and a bit of a lunatic", never happier than when he has something to grumble about. It seems unlikely, but the point is he quite likes being a killjoy and it is working for his squad.

Talking about their mentality before the game he said: "I'm probably having to do less and less from that point of view," then added: "but sometimes it's nice when they give you a reason to,"

Did you know with a Digital subscription to Yorkshire Post, you can get access to all of our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

His players spoilt his fun when they dropped two points against the Hatters on Tuesday but they did give him something to work with.

Now Jack Wilshere's Luton have got their head around being in League One - something which took Huddersfield and takes most big clubs time (take note any Sheffield Wednesday fans still expecting to cruise promotion) - outplaying them is pretty difficult. Huddersfield managed it.

Drury tried to go all Michael Vaughan 2005 with his "look at them celebrating a draw," schtick, not knowing Wilshere had told the room minutes earlier post-match on-pitch huddles were becoming a thing for them.

But it was true Luton ought to have been more grateful for the point, and accurate or not, the narrative can help Town, which is all Drury is interested in.

Yet one piece of awful defending and an inability to turn threatening attacking play into cast-iron chances meant they had to settle for a 1-1 draw. Drury was obviously not jumping through hoops about that.

But seeing his players so down in the dumps about it perked him up, even if nobody thought to tell his facial expression.

"I just said to the players in the dressing room, every game will pose a new challenge and it gives us something else to learn from," said the first-time head coach, whose contradictions include being a compelling interviewee who does not enjoy speaking to the media.

"I'm disappointed we've not won but I liked that we had to come from behind. I liked that we were disappointed with the goal we conceded.

"Overall, I thought it was a good performance but there's key moments for us to learn from and that's what Luton gave us - some things to learn from. That's what we need because we want to keep growing and getting better.

"We need use that now as fuel to motivate us to get better."

Whilst Huddersfield played well between the boxes, it was bad football, not bad luck, which stopped them winning. The goal they conceded to Jordan Clark in the first half was awful, Sean Roughan coming out to win a long ball and not doing so, Town cutting out Jamie Donely's cross but Iban Bowat's header not getting it clear, and no on closing the former Barnsley player down before he hit the net.

At the other end, although Derensili Sanches Fernandes hit a post with a deflected from-kick and Joe Taylor almost pulled the net out of the ground with a volleyed equaliser which took him to 10 goals for the season, and 23 for the year, there was not enough goal threat.

Bali Mumba regularly wriggling down the line, Fernandes forcing Luton to swap Isiah Jones to the opposite side to try to deal with him, Cameron Humphreys twice going close to scoring direct from corners and Taylor even attempting the volley showed a team as confident as one top of the table and now unbeaten in eight games should be.

"I thought we were absolutely dominant in the first half, got to their box numerous times, hit the post," said Drury, taking care not to back up his words with anything as indulgent as a smile.

"We probably just lacked that little bit of decision-making in key moments. I felt we were trying to almost find the perfect angle to shoot and not use their defenders to help us, whether it be through the legs or shape one in. The goal is a very poor goal to concede. We have to do better in that moment because no disrespect, but that is not a well-worked goal. That is a goal that we've not defended well enough or we would normally concede.

"Both as individual players and the group as a whole, I thought there was a real strong mentality to kick on from there.

"I don't look at any of my players and think they didn't have a good game.

"I can't pick out players who didn't perform.

"But I think it probably tells you where we are as a group (that) everyone's got their head down in the changing room and it's flat because we've taken a point and we want three."

You can never be 100 per cent sure but it felt like Mr Miserable probably went to bed on Tuesday pretty happy.

Ontario house by Brian O'Brian takes cues from farm buildings

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Canadian architect Brian O’Brian has completed a rural weekend house in Ontario that features a slanted, corrugated-metal facade and an angled roof covered with greenery.

The Hemlock House sits on a 72-acre (29-hectare) property in the southern town of Markdale.

The project grew in scope over time. Originally, the plan was to renovate an old farmhouse, but the client ultimately decided to create an entirely new house.

“What started as a modest renovation evolved into a full replacement of the small, 1930s farmhouse,” said Brian O’Brian, a Toronto-based architect.

The commission came about after the client saw another project by O’Brian in the area – a 19th-century schoolhouse with stone walls that had been converted into a cottage.

For the Hemlock House, O’Brian took cues from the region’s agrarian buildings to conceive the design.

Set on a flat site surrounded by woods and farmland, the house is composed of a long bar and adjoining box, and totals 3,250 square feet (302 square metres).

A long entry driveway “traces the lineage of the site”, passing by an old farmhouse and century-old barn.

The new house draws upon these earlier buildings and “extends the language of agricultural buildings into a contemporary domestic form”, the architect said.

The home’s bar-shaped volume is topped with a sloped, metal-clad roof, while the attached box has an angular green roof.

The north side of the house is lined with a slanted wall made of corrugated metal.

Cut into the wall are three windows, which can be covered by external, perforated steel panels that slide up and down. This elevation also features a boxy protrusion with wood cladding.

“This is meant to feel like an agrarian building at first view, but one that begins softening and opening on approach,” said the architect.

The southern elevation – which overlooks patios and a swimming pool – features hemlock siding and ample glazing. Various roof overhangs help shield the building from sun and snow.

The house also has a carport, which is sheltered by a canopy made of wood and metal.

Within the home, there is a division between public and private spaces.

The linear volume, which spans two levels, contains a principal suite, a secondary suite, and two bedrooms with a shared bathroom. The adjoining box holds a kitchen, dining area and living room.

Rooms feature neutral colours and a mix of earthy and industrial materials.

“Concrete and hemlock define every surface, carrying a steady material logic through the house,” the architect said.

Other projects in Ontario include a 1970s chalet that was renovated and expanded by UUfie to conform to its steep site, and a black cabin by Barbora Vokac Taylor that includes wedge-shaped clerestory windows.

Anthropologie Daily Practice Review: I Tested the Brand’s New Loungewear Label

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

The laidback line offers cozy fleeces, elevated sweats, and layerable outerwear.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

When I think of Anthropologie, I think of its versatile wardrobe staples like denim, office-appropriate blouses, and midi-length dresses. But the brand offers so much more, from home goods and furniture to wedding collections and statement accessories; I’ve bought a smattering of SKUs from Anthropologie over the years.

However, I was only recently introduced to its newest label, Daily Practice, which launched in summer 2024. The laid-back loungewear line combines style, comfort, and versatility for busy women who do it all.

Daily Practice is Anthropologie’s loungewear label, offering styles for everything from downtime to playtime to working at home. It’s less “activewear” and more “cozy athleisure” meant to multitask in our everyday lives. Think: sherpa fleeces, sweats, puffer jackets, casual tees, and stretchy dresses.

Ahead of Daily Practice’s latest collection, Anthropologie reached out to Women’s Health to try some pieces from the launch. Over the past two weeks, I tested four styles: a fleece quarter-zip, balloon-style joggers, a mock neck pullover sweater, and straight-leg sweatpants. Since New York is having transitional fall weather, I wore each piece on its own and layered with base layers like T-shirts and long sleeves. I also wore some styles while working from home, at the movie theater, and spectating at the New York City Marathon.

Of everything in the new collection, I was most excited to try this quarter-zip sherpa jacket. A fleece is one of the best layers to have in your fall and winter wardrobe. On warmer days, I wore it with just a T-shirt underneath, but as the weather gets colder, I plan to layer it with turtlenecks and wool base layers.

The High Altitude Sherpa is super soft, easy to layer under, and versatile thanks to the side vents, front pockets, and tall funnel neck. I love that the neck zips up to my chin, so I can cozy up on cold days.

The block print pattern stands out in all three colorways, but I especially love the turquoise one for a bright pop of color and the orange contrast stitching. I opted for size small for a relaxed, boxy fit, and it fits true to size.

These laid-back pants look just as good styled with ballet flats and a cardigan as they do lounging on my couch. The lightweight, breathable cotton material has just enough spandex to make the joggers feel buttery smooth against the skin. Note: The pants are fairly thin, so they’re not suitable for freezing temps, but since they’re baggy, you can easily layer leggings or thermal underwear underneath.

I prefer the Ease State Pants when I’m working from home and don’t want to feel like I’m sitting in sweats all day. They’re also great for light movement like yoga, walking, or running errands. I’d suggest taking your normal size in these pants for that balloon-leg look—the drawstring waist will ensure a perfect fit.

Designed with a relaxed silhouette and a ribbed mock neck, this cotton-blend knit pullover feels like a sweatshirt but looks much more put together. I love the dropped shoulders for an easygoing look you can dress up or down. Contrasting ribbed trim along the sleeves and cuffs adds style and stretch.

This style definitely runs large (I ordered a size small), and I found excessive fabric around the bottom of the torso that didn’t drape the way I wanted. However, as a tall person with long arms, I appreciate the sleeve length. The mock neck is also comfortable to wear all day without itching or feeling tight.

In theory, I love these pants so much. They’re the perfect mix of sweatpants and trousers, and the material feels like a blend of track pants and cotton-fleece sweats. The straight-leg silhouette and front darts create a more tailored look with hidden pockets concealed in the dart seam. Two large back pockets also fit your phone and wallet.

My only complaint is that the waist runs very large. I wish there was a drawstring or more elastic in the waistband because they’re too large to stay up on my hips. I do like the low-rise style, which offers a more trendy look that’s great for layering, but I would size down next time. For now, I haven’t worn them out of the house since they’re too big, but they’ll be great for layering over bike shorts or thermal leggings this winter.

Daily Practice knows its audience: working women, multi-tasking moms, stylish consumers who care about looking cute and feeling comfy. If this sounds like you, the new collection is definitely worth checking out. The label has a great range of elevated basics, loungewear, and layerable outerwear right now.

While the price points are steeper than some other loungewear brands, I found that the designs have more thought and intention behind them than your typical “leggings and a sports bra” outfitter. The cute silhouettes, colors, and details make Daily Practice’s pieces feel fashionable.

Whether I’m at home watching a movie or on the go, I know I’ll reach for these versatile pieces a lot over the next couple of months. Similar to Anthropologie’s other in-house labels, Daily Practice runs large. So if you’re between sizes, I would recommend sizing down.

Sidney Lee is a shopping editor at Hearst Magazines, covering fashion, beauty, lifestyle, and more for Women’s Health, Prevention, and Oprah Daily. She has a knack for turning internet trends, viral moments, and must-have products into smart recommendations for readers. You can also read her work on Best Products and Runner's World.

Shannen Zitz is the Associate Style & Reviews Editor at Women's Health, where she covers all-things sneakers and activewear. Previously, she was an assistant editor at Prevention. Shannen graduated from the State University of New York at Cortland with a bachelor's degree in English. If she’s not reading or writing, you can probably find her frequenting the skincare and makeup forums on Reddit or hogging the squat rack at the gym.

CVS Health plans to split itself up. Here's why a breakup could make sense now

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

CVS Health plans to break itself up — and this time, the bet looks a lot more viable.

The healthcare conglomerate on Wednesday announced plans to split into two publicly traded companies by the first half of 2028. CVS would separate its pharmacy business – including roughly 9,000 retail locations – from its major insurance business and pharmacy benefit manager, Caremark.

The move comes more than two years after CVS considered a breakup but ultimately walked away from the idea. At the time, analysts questioned whether the retail pharmacy business could survive on its own.

“Back then, the idea of retail standing alone was unthinkable,” Brian Tanquilut, senior equity research analyst at Jefferies, told CNBC.

But the economics of the businesses have changed: CVS's retail pharmacy business has become more consistently profitable, while its insurer, Aetna, has improved its performance despite persistently high medical costs in Medicare Advantage plans. That could make it easier for the company to separate the units — and for investors to recognize the value of each on its own, rather than valuing them together as one sprawling healthcare conglomerate.

“For us, it's showing the stability of the pharmacy,” Michael Cherny, senior research analyst at Leerink Partners, said in an interview. “This business is a much different business from the one that's been considered three years ago, five years ago, 10 years ago, where there had been a litany of headwinds that the pharmacy just couldn't overcome.”

CVS CEO David Joyner told CNBC’s "Squawk on the Street" on Wednesday that back in 2024, the company was "in a turnaround" without the flexibility, cash or business performance to execute a split. Now, CVS has posted eight consecutive quarters of beating Wall Street earnings estimates and has raised its guidance several times over the last two years, thanks to improvement across its PBM, pharmacy and insurer.

Since taking the helm in 2024, Joyner has pursued an aggressive turnaround plan that involves $2 billion in cost cuts, store closures, leadership shakeups and efforts to rein in Medicare Advantage costs.

The company also now expects to generate at least $15.5 billion in operating cash flow in 2026, a roughly 70% increase from $9.1 billion in 2024, and says it has reached its leverage target ahead of schedule.

"What's changed in two years is the fact that we now can look at this business and the assets that are assembled and really determine, without any distress or without anything that we have to do, what is the right strategic decision to make," Joyner told analysts on a call Wednesday, adding that the decision comes "out of strength."

While investors have largely cheered CVS's progress in recent years, Wall Street appeared more pessimistic about Wednesday's announcement, as the company's shares dropped about 12%.

CVS's retail business has benefited from changes in the competitive landscape, including the widespread retrenchment and store closures of Walgreens, Rite Aid and other pharmacy operators over the last few years. That’s created opportunities for CVS to gain market share, some analysts told CNBC.

CVS has also introduced changes to its pharmacy reimbursement model. Its CostVantage program is designed to replace the traditional process with a more transparent, cost-based model. Some analysts say that has helped the company better align reimbursement with the cost of dispensing prescriptions and contributed to steadier profits.

Those developments represent a meaningful shift for a business that has faced years of pressure from declining prescription reimbursement, store closures and competition from online pharmacies and other retailers.

That doesn't mean the retail pharmacy has become a high-growth business. CVS's retail pharmacy segment remains a relatively low-margin operation, with adjusted operating margins of roughly 4% to 5%. But analysts say it may now be stable and profitable enough to operate independently – even if investors value it at a discount to CVS's other businesses.

In a note Wednesday, RBC Capital Markets analyst Ben Hendrix said the lower valuation investors assign to the retail pharmacy unit has weighed on CVS's overall value and obscured the earnings strength of businesses like Aetna and Caremark.

The underlying growth of the insurance and PBM businesses is also stronger than that of retail pharmacy, Tanquilut said, after accounting for changes to Caremark's rebate arrangements.

Aetna in particular has gotten a better handle on medical costs by exiting unprofitable markets, expanding value-based care models, and tightening utilization management.

The breakup also comes as Caremark faces mounting scrutiny over PBM practices, including state-level legal and regulatory challenges involving the businesses and their relationships with pharmacies.

Republicans in Tennessee and Arkansas have passed laws that would ban any one company from owning both a pharmacy and a PBM — thought the Arkansas measure has been held up in court. Cherny noted that legal hurdles involving PBMs in other states have continued to pile up.

“Through what seems to be a never-ending litany of state lawsuits, it feels like it's been under more pressure,” Cherny said. “It's unclear if it just became, for lack of a better term, too tenuous to move forward with those two entities combined as one. I’m not sure.”

Even so, Joyner told the Wall Street Journal that the company wasn’t splitting up because of regulatory issues. CVS has said the separation follows a strategic review and is intended to give each business greater focus and flexibility.

The move also marks a significant departure from the integrated healthcare strategy CVS spent years building.

CVS spent roughly $70 billion to acquire Aetna in 2018 and argued that combining health insurance, pharmacy benefit management, retail pharmacies and health providers under one roof would help coordinate care and create a competitive advantage. CVS was still promoting that model as recently as its investor day last year.

The proposed split suggests the company now sees more value in separating at least some of those businesses – though it is not abandoning vertical integration altogether.

Aetna and Caremark will remain together in the healthcare company, alongside CVS’s specialty pharmacy, its primary care clinic Oak Street Health and its home-visit provider Signify Health. The separate pharmacy company will include roughly 9,000 retail stores, mail pharmacy operations and MinuteClinic, a retail medical clinic located inside certain CVS locations.

Both sides are large enough that they can stand on their own, Cherny said. Caremark is one of the country's three largest PBMs, while CVS has one of the largest retail pharmacy footprints in the U.S.

But separating the company raises questions about how much of the commercial relationship between the businesses can survive.

The relationship has historically benefited both sides. As it stands, Caremark can use CVS's extensive retail footprint to offer members convenient access to prescriptions and steer them toward CVS locations through preferred pharmacy arrangements. Programs such as Maintenance Choice, for example, give eligible members the option to fill certain maintenance medications at CVS retail pharmacies or through mail order.

Those arrangements also helped direct prescription volume to CVS's stores, giving the retail pharmacy unit an important source of customers. Because the businesses shared a parent company, the financial benefits of that relationship ultimately went to CVS as a whole.

In a note, RBC's Hendrix also raised questions about how the split could impact Aetna's Medicare Advantage quality ratings, which determine whether health plans qualify for bonus payments from the government. Retail pharmacists have played a role in engaging Aetna members, and a separation could complicate that relationship.

But Joyner told analysts Wednesday that the synergy between the two businesses would be "preserved in commercial agreements between the two organizations," adding that "we don't need the integration of an operating structure."

Joyner said CVS will be working on those potential agreements over the next 15 to 18 months.

Preferred pharmacy networks are common across the industry, and separate ownership does not prevent Caremark from continuing to favor CVS pharmacies through negotiated arrangements, Cherny said. The difference is that under separate ownership, the profits from those arrangements would no longer accrue to a single company, he noted.

“The issue is not whether the companies can continue working together in some capacity, but whether any future contractual arrangements preserve the SAME competitive advantages and flexibility as today under common ownership,” Cherny said in an afternoon note on Wednesday.

Still, both businesses face challenges on their own.

Tanquilut cautioned that some of CVS's recent retail pharmacy gains have come as Walgreens and other competitors have closed stores, raising questions about whether the growth can be sustained. The business also remains exposed to consumer pressure and competition from online pharmacies.

Caremark, meanwhile, faces continued regulatory scrutiny and potential headwinds from changes to the federal 340B drug discount program, which requires drugmakers to offer discounted medications to eligible hospitals and other healthcare providers serving low-income and vulnerable patients.

CVS participates in the program by providing pharmacy services to those organizations, but tweaks to manufacturer data-sharing policies and potential reimbursement changes could weigh on that business in 2027, Tanquilut said.

Aetna, meanwhile, is still working through a multiyear plan to rebuild its margins, as it looks to improve the profitability of its Medicare Advantage business.

Ultimately, CVS is betting that the value of letting investors assess its businesses separately will outweigh the benefits of keeping them under one roof. The split will test whether each can thrive independently.

Muriqi caps Fenerbahce comeback to make Liverpool pay for defensive lapses

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Andoni Iraola arrived in Istanbul seeking improvement from Liverpool’s attack but departed with sudden cause for concern at the opposite end of the pitch. Fenerbahce ignited Dirk Kuyt’s interim spell with a tumultuous victory after Liverpool detonated their 100% Champions League record with several defensive lapses.

Liverpool’s second successive defeat left Iraola exasperated for entirely different reasons to Sunday’s loss to Arsenal. Blame was found only in the mirror this time around. It was so careless and so unnecessary.

The visitors led twice, through Trey Nyoni’s first senior goal and a header from substitute Alexander Isak, but Fenerbahce twice hit back quickly and snatched victory when Vedat Muriqi emerged as the strongest player from a pack of red shirts. Fenerbahce’s first goal came from a free-kick, their second from a corner and their third from a long throw-in.

“Cheap goals, messy goals,” as the Liverpool head coach put it. “We knew set pieces were one of the strengths of Fenerbahce. We have been dealing with them well, both this season and today, it has been something we have been working on since pre-season, but it cost us today. The last goal was maybe because they are strong and tall but the other two we have to control better.”

Ronald Araújo resting on the bench offers one explanation for Liverpool’s weak defensive display but does not cover every fault. The visitors enjoyed large spells of control and, despite an anonymous display from Bradley Barcola as the central striker, should never have given hope to an ageing, average Fenerbahce side packed with familiar names from the Premier League.

As expected, Turkish supporters tested the eardrums with incessant whistling whenever Liverpool took possession. They were a test for structural engineers too with their constant bouncing in the upper tier of the home end. Liverpool sought to dilute the atmosphere by controlling possession. Rio Ngumoha was prominent and N’Golo Kanté should have been booked for halting one promising run by leaping on the teenager’s back. Referee Clément Turpin was in a lenient mood with both sides.

Fenerbahce had struggled to hold up play in the Liverpool half or create a chance of note when Archie Brown exposed the visiting defence with a superb ball from left-back. The pass sailed over the struggling Kostas Tsimikas and was controlled well by Mason Greenwood, before he was pushed over by the Liverpool defender. Neither Turpin nor the video assistant referee deemed it a penalty kick.

The decibel levels cranked up and went even higher when Liverpool took the lead moments later. Ngumoha was heavily involved with a darting run and shot that was charged down into the path of Dominik Szoboszlai. Brown, under pressure from Virgil van Dijk, headed the midfielder’s cross clear but only as far as Nyoni. The young midfielder swept a low volley beyond Ederson with the aid of a slight deflection off Kojo Peprah Oppong. It was a memorable milestone in the continued rise of Nyoni; the first goal of his career at the highest level.

It was also the cue for Fenerbahce to rouse themselves. The hosts almost levelled straight from the restart when Brown’s low centre was turned against Alisson’s crossbar by Irfan Can Kahveci, who may have been ruled offside. They did not have to wait long for the equaliser. After Szoboszlai was penalised for a foul on Kanté, Greenwood’s resulting free-kick took a huge deflection off Jérémy Jacquet’s stomach and sailed past the wrongfooted Alisson. The Liverpool keeper did not hide his anger at Jacquet turning away from the free-kick and leaving a costly hole in the wall.

Kick off your evenings with the Guardian's take on the world of football

The second half mirrored the first with Liverpool in control until scoring and provoking a reaction from the Turkish side. Substitutes Cody Gakpo and Isak had only been on the pitch three minutes when they combined to restore the visitors’ advantage, the Swedish striker heading past Ederson from Gakpo’s left-wing cross.

But Fenerbahce again levelled swiftly when Kanté swept in a cross following a corner and Gakpo, attempting to head clear, glanced the ball away from Alisson’s grasp to give Brown a simple tap-in at the back post. “In the stadium it was almost impossible to communicate,” Iraola admitted. “It was difficult for me to give instructions and the communication must have been difficult between Cody and Ali.”

Worse was to come for Liverpool when Birmingham-born Brown looped a cross into the heart of Liverpool’s box. Muriqi, a second-half replacement for Romelu Lukaku, managed to hold off three Liverpool players – Jacquet, Alexis Mac Allister and Nyoni – and plant a header into the bottom corner. Iraola added: “It’s so frustrating because this is a difficult place to play and we were winning twice. We need to look at how we conceded the goals.”

US Bank Regulator Issues First AI Framework as Banks Battle Rogue Agents

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

US Bank Regulator Issues First AI Framework as Banks Battle Rogue Agents

The U.S. Office of the Comptroller of the Currency issued its first supervisory framework for artificial intelligence in banking today, telling examiners to focus less on how powerful an AI system is and more on what happens when it makes a mistake.

The OCC's framework addresses that very concern, although the regulator has not linked its issuance to those specific incidents.

In a section covering operational resilience, the agency warns that external agents operated by customers, fintechs, or other businesses could create risks for banks even when the banks don't control those systems.

It suggests examiners consider measures including identity verification, delegated permissions, transaction limits, monitoring, and the ability to suspend or revoke access.

The guidelines aim to tackle the dilemma of how to let customers use AI assistants to manage their finances without giving autonomous software unrestricted access to accounts that banks are already facing.

The new OCC guidance arrives less than two weeks after reports that major banks were discovering unauthorized third-party AI agents operating inside customer accounts, sometimes taking actions that appeared to go beyond users' instructions.

Morgan Stanley was blocking outside agents, while JPMorgan, Bank of America, and Citi were restricting access, according to an Axios report on October 22. Bank executives described the situation as "horrifying," raising questions about who controls an autonomous agent once it has access to someone's money.

The OCC's framework makes a distinction between an AI system's technical sophistication and the damage it could cause.

A simple agent authorized to initiate financial transactions may present greater risk than a more advanced model limited to writing internal documents, the regulator said.

Under the 22-page framework, examiners can consider the consequences of failure, whether an action can be reversed, and how much control humans retain over the system.

The agency outlines four illustrative risk tiers. Fully autonomous payment agents and real-time credit decision systems fall into the highest category, while basic internal productivity tools occupy the lowest.

Higher-risk systems may warrant tighter restrictions on autonomy, independent testing, and human approval before consequential actions can proceed.

The framework also addresses AI systems that continue operating while making harmful decisions. Suggested safeguards include circuit breakers, rate limits, restricted permissions, and emergency shutdown controls.

Those considerations are especially relevant to the outside agents described in Axios's earlier reporting, where banks struggled to distinguish legitimate customer-authorized software from systems operating beyond their intended permissions.

The OCC stressed that the document does not create new regulatory requirements or enforceable standards for banks.

Instead, it gives examiners a way to evaluate AI within existing operational, compliance, and cybersecurity risk categories. Banks are not required to establish a separate AI risk category.

The document uses the mandatory term "Super Intelligence," which the Donald Trump administration insists on using in place of “artificial intelligence,” but its scope includes conventional AI and “autonomous agents” rather than only hypothetical systems with intelligence beyond human capabilities.

The framework covers AI used in payments, fraud detection, customer service, and other banking operations. It also extends its risk considerations to external AI agents connecting through bank channels.

The OCC said it intends to update the guidance as the technology develops. For now, its illustrative risk scale puts autonomous payment agents in the highest-risk category, regardless of how well they perform in testing.

‘You Can See Everything’ … in Space? A24 Sends Elizabeth Holmes Doc to ISS

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Nathan Fielder's documentary about the former Theranos CEO will screen for astronauts in outer space.

The popularity of You Can See Everything has apparently surpassed Earthly measures. Nearly three weeks after the nearly 3-hour documentary about disgraced former Theranos CEO Elizabeth Holmes premiered in theaters domestically, studio A24 is screening it in outer space.

The studio said in an A24 Instagram post that it has granted NASA’s request to screen the film for astronauts aboard the International Space Station.

A representative for A24 didn’t immediately respond to a request for comment.

I’d say this was surprising news, yet after I saw the impact the movie had on Halloween, it makes perfect sense. You Can See Everything is a phenomenon.

At my daughter’s school, I saw a toddler dressed as Holmes, which certainly was a choice. People all over my social feeds were costumed as the infamous scrambled-egg bowl from the movie (IYKYK). Heck, the friend I saw the movie with took my idea and dressed in all black, wearing old-school headphones and an emo hairstyle, and carried a laptop to channel Nathan Fielder.

You Can See Everything, co-directed by Nathan Fielder and Lance Oppenheim, was initially slated for a limited theatrical release in the US. After three weeks, the documentary hit No. 1 at the box office last weekend, beating DC’s Clayface for the top spot.

Now, audiences around the world are clamoring to get a taste of the movie.

They still have to wait, though. UK moviegoers won’t be able to see it until Nov. 20. It’s coming to France on Dec. 30, and while Sony Pictures acquired the rights to release You Can See Everything in countries like Germany, South Korea and Latin America, no dates have been announced yet.

That said, those lucky astronauts in low Earth orbit will soon be side-eyeing yellow mixing bowls, questioning every move made by Holmes’s partner, Billy Evans, and simply wanting more Amanda Seyfried in their lives.

For a movie that is already super disorienting, one has to assume that removing gravity from the equation will make watching You Can See Everything a next-level viewing experience.

Tesla Allegedly Misled Thousands Of South Korean Customers About 'Full Self-Driving'

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Usually, when we tell you that Tesla has gotten into hot water for misleading folks about its "Full Self-Driving" Level 2 driver assistance system, it has to do with Tesla overstating just how capable of a system it is. That's not the case in South Korea, though, where the Texas-based automaker has allegedly vastly overstated the legality of FSD usage and charged customers for it nonetheless. Now, tens of thousands of Korean Tesla owners are looking for answers.

Tesla apparently markets FSD in South Korea despite the fact that it knows the majority of its cars can't legally use the program in the country, the complaint from an advocacy group alleges. That means it can't be activated on the car. Soon, the complaint will be filed with the Korean Fair Trade Commission, a consumer protection agency, according to Reuters. Tesla will have to answer for why it marketed FSD to prospective buyers for years, even collecting the $6,726 lump-sum payment required for activation, even though it couldn't legally be used.

Currently, just 20% of Teslas registered in South Korea can legally use FSD because they're built in the U.S., according to a preliminary testimony given by Tesla Korea country manager Seo Young-deuk last month. Those specific cars meet U.S. vehicle-safety standards that South Korea recognizes as part of a free trade agreement. The thing is, most Teslas on the road in South Korea are manufactured in China, where no such agreement exists. It was only recently that Tesla started the process of obtaining FSD certification on China-made vehicles, even though it has been charging customers for the software for over three years.

Right now, it's not totally clear how many customers in South Korea bought FSD subscriptions on ineligible vehicles, but the automaker has sold over 170,000 China-made vehicles in the country since 2023, Reuters reports. The company continued to market FSD as sales of ineligible China-manufactured vehicles surged in South Korea in recent years, and last year, 99% of new Teslas sold in the country couldn't legally use FSD.

The complaint alleges that last year, Tesla promoted FSD as "coming soon" and posted a demonstration video filmed in South Korea without clearly disclosing that the tech could only be used in a tiny fraction of the vehicles it was selling, according to Reuters. The consumer advocacy group accused Tesla Korea of deceptive marketing and has asked the KFTC to issue a temporary cease-and-desist order on marketing FSD for ineligible vehicles, impose fines and refer the case to state investigators.

It may be an uphill battle, though. Just last month, a court in Seoul dismissed a lawsuit filed by Tesla owners seeking refunds for the FSD option, Reuters reports. The court rejected the claim that Tesla had failed to deliver the driver-assistance system. Instead, it put the onus of the issue on regulatory and legal restrictions that had limited the company's ability to roll out future functions.

Man, that feels like one weak ahh argument to me.

New Flutter CEO bets on FanDuel promotions to win customers as profits suffer

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Flutter's new CEO Dan Taylor is counting on better products and more generous customer rewards to help convert FanDuel's improving engagement into stronger profits.

Taylor's been at the helm for just five weeks and issued his first earnings report as CEO on Wednesday, with third-quarter revenue and adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization missing Wall Street's expectations.

The company's stock is down more than 60% year to date.

Yet Taylor says the company is now on the right track.

"We made some changes to the product," Taylor told CNBC in an interview. "Fundamentally, the way you win this business is by delighting customers, delivering the very best experiences and delivering to them in the way that they want, with the right overall proposition."

FanDuel also launched a nationwide rewards program in September that Taylor said has "resonated really, really well."

The early customer numbers are improving. Average monthly players on FanDuel's U.S. sportsbook rose 9% in the third quarter, while bet counts increased 15%, Taylor said.

Flutter's earnings release showed total U.S. average monthly players up 12%, including 24% growth in iGaming. Sportsbook handle, or the total amount wagered, rose 13%, but sportsbook revenue grew just 4% as promotional spending increased.

Taylor said the company plans to keep investing in customer experience. He said Flutter sees room to improve its structural margins over time by changing the mix of products customers use.

Flutter has increased its media spend to build FanDuel Predicts, its predictions platform to rival Kalshi and Polymarket, but the cost of sales as a percent of revenue is forecast to be 58% for the full-year 2026 and into 2027.

Taylor said there's fierce industry competition for customers but even still, FanDuel trading volume has skyrocketed 271% since July.

The FanDuel Predicts app added 140,000 new customers in October, Taylor said.

Taylor also defended the long-term opportunity in the U.S., pointing out about half the country still does not have access to regulated sports betting and 90% lacks regulated online gaming.

He argued that expanding legal markets is not a partisan issue because regulated betting offers consumer protections and gives tax revenue to states.

For the third quarter, Flutter reported an adjusted loss of 18 cents per share, compared with analysts' expectations for a loss of 28 cents, according to LSEG. Revenue rose 2% to $3.87 billion, below the roughly $4 billion LSEG consensus, while adjusted EBITDA fell roughly 25% year over year to $360 million, also below expectations.

The company posted a net loss of $1.15 billion, including an $873 million non-cash impairment tied primarily to Brazil, which banned sports betting and online gaming in September.

Taylor said the company was disappointed by Brazil's provisional measure, but took the impairment to "set ourselves up to win long-term."

The complications in Brazil and unfavorable sports results in the U.S. and internationally led Flutter to cut its full-year revenue midpoint by $300 million to $17.61 billion and lower its 2026 adjusted EBITDA outlook by $160 million to nearly $2.5 billion.

In the meantime, capital returns are on hold: Taylor said Flutter needs to bring leverage back into its target range of 2 to 2.5 times over roughly the next 24 months before it can consider buybacks again. For now, he said, the priority is organic growth and paying down debt.

— CNBC's Mallory Leonard contributed to this report.

‘Soft’ – Stephen Warnock takes aim at Liverpool star who ‘cost his side’ against Fenerbahce

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Stephen Warnock was critical of one Liverpool player over the concession of a ‘soft goal’ in their Champions League clash against Fenerbahce on Wednesday.

The Reds had the lead twice at the Sukru Saracoglu Stadium, with Trey Nyoni and Alexander Isak netting for Andoni Iraola’s side, but the midfielder’s goal was swiftly cancelled out by a deflected Mason Greenwood free kick.

The home side equalised again through Archie Brown and had the final say with a Vedat Muriqi header which secured all three points.

The second Fenerbahce goal had Warnock pointing the finger of blame at Alisson Becker for not coming to claim the ball from the cross which led to the left-back’s leveller.

Speaking on MOTD: UEFA Champions League for BBC Sport, the former Liverpool defender said: “That’s another soft goal conceded by Liverpool but this one was more down to Alisson not coming to claim the ball – and either cleaning out Gakpo or letting him know he is coming.

“I just watched the replay and there was definitely no shout… that’s a lack of concentration from the goalkeeper, and it has cost his side.”

Want more Empire of the Kop coverage? Add us as a preferred source on Google to your favourites list for news you can trust

Alisson had bailed out his teammates a couple of times earlier in the match after some sloppy errors just outside the penalty area, but the goalkeeper won’t be happy with his involvement in Brown’s equaliser.

He was unusually hesitant in coming for the ball as the cross was played in, although he doesn’t bear sole culpability for a cheap goal for Fenerbahce.

Cody Gakpo was unlucky that his attempted headed clearance went backwards, but Dominik Szoboszlai completely switched off to allow the left-back to run in behind him and have a simple finish from close range.

It was a collective mess-up from Liverpool, who could also have done a lot more to prevent Muriqi from scoring what proved to be the winner for Dirk Kuyt’s side.

It’s now two defeats in a row to begin November for the Reds, but they’re still in a healthy position to advance to the round of 16 in the Champions League, having won their three previous games in the tournament.

These Are the Coolest and Craziest Cars We Saw at SEMA 2026

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

From modified Bugattis to hand-sawed pickups, SEMA draws the most exciting builds in the world each year. These are our favorites.

It’s impossible to grasp the size of the SEMA show until you actually experience it. There are thousands of builds created by tens of thousands of dedicated enthusiasts excited to share their passion with the world. While we would love to spend the full week delving into each and every build, there simply isn’t enough time. So, we picked a few of our early favorites from this year’s show. Hopefully, you’ll find them as endearing as we do.

Kei cars are already an adorable bunch, but add in Rocket Bunny’s Pandem Pocket Bunny kit, and now you’re talking best-in-show cute at your local car show. What you’re looking at here is a pair of Suzuki Twins built into R32 and R34 GT-Rs. These specific examples were built with no expense spared. The R32 makes around 120 horsepower from its turbocharged 1.0-liter, while the R34’s Twin was left more stock. –Jack Fitzgerald

See all results for used cars for sale near 90011

I found this chopped-up Tesla Cybertruck parked in the back corner of the Central Hall, covered in Grand Theft Auto VI and Rockstar Games branding. Somehow, with a sports car figure and a giant APR spoiler, the Cybertruck’s styling looks good? –Carter Fry

Can’t help but grin looking at this track-spec first-gen Toyota Tacoma. Not pictured is the engine mounted out back in the bed, below the massive rear wing. –Carter Fry

No, you’re not looking at some AI hallucination of a Lexus pickup that doesn’t exist. This is the Meso Customs TX1, a legitimate Lexus pickup based on the Toyota Tacoma and built by the crew at Meso Customs. The front end takes on the look of a Lexus GX, and even the interior gets overhauled to look and feel like what you’d get from the luxury brand. –Jack Fitzgerald

There are plenty of ways to get your race car to the track, but most of them involve purpose-built haulers based on proven HD trucks. Builder Raymond Canales looked at the available options and said, “I can do better.” Then he set to work creating this tag-axle VW hauler based on the beloved Type 2, giving new meaning to its “Transporter” surname. Built specifically to transport his VW Beetle race car, the Type 2 Hauler is just the kind of inspired lunacy with a practical purpose that we can get behind. –Andrew Wendler

Mark Denlinger’s Mad Customs is responsible for this Chevy wagon build. While Nomads get most of the love, this 210 four-door wagon restomod features a natural patina to complement its subtle hardware updates. Yes, it’s lowered, but so deftly it almost appears to just be sitting on a worn suspension; the Forgeline EO1 wheels are painted black to keep a low profile; keep the hood closed, and you’d never see its LT4 GM V-8. Denlinger also fabbed up custom tubs to clear the rear tires and modified the cargo floor to clear the custom exhaust that was crafted in-house. With apologies to Michael Jackson, this is one smooth criminal—if you know where to look. –Andrew Wendler

See all results for used cars for sale near 90011

Steve Strope has a history of clean builds, and this Z/28 does nothing to sully that legacy. Featuring a carbureted Chevy big-block and a massaged turbo-hydromantic 400 transmission with a Gear Vendors overdrive, it pays respect to builds of the past. But the craftsmanship is so meticulous, and the Ice Gray Metallic paint and Graphite Blue interior are so sublime, the whole thing makes a contemporary statement without trying too hard. The Camaro was basically the poster child for custom builds in the ‘80s and ‘90s, but for those of us raised in the Rust Belt, there’s still something special about a well-executed 1969 Z/28 project. –Andrew Wendler

Jack Fitzgerald’s love for cars stems from his as yet unshakable addiction to Formula 1. After a brief stint as a detailer for a local dealership group in college, he knew he needed a more permanent way to drive all the new cars he couldn’t afford and decided to pursue a career in auto writing. By hounding his college professors at the University of Wisconsin-Milwaukee, he was able to travel Wisconsin seeking out stories in the auto world before landing his dream job at Car and Driver. His new goal is to delay the inevitable demise of his 2010 Volkswagen Golf.
